Yeah, hi… I guess the main reason I came in is I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. It’s like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body still feels heavy, and my head feels like it’s dragging before the day even starts.

Yeah, I have noticed that some. It’s not like I can’t focus on anything at all, but my mind wanders when I’m trying to do something that should be pretty straightforward.

Like at work, at the register, I’ll be looking right at the screen and doing the transaction, and then I realize I’ve kind of lost the thread because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever is hanging over me. Then I have to stop and double-check myself, or recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some small mistake before it becomes a bigger thing.

It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’m there, I’m looking at it, but part of my brain is somewhere else.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I’d say I’ve been feeling anxious pretty often. It’s kind of like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for very long, but not in a good way. It jumps around from worry to worry.

Like I’ll be thinking about money, and then before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting stress on the house, and then I start worrying about my wife being stressed. Then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, or something I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into, “Am I falling behind? Am I letting people down?” And then at work I’ll start wondering if I’m messing something up or if people can tell I’m not fully on top of things.

So yeah, nervous is probably the right word. It’s not always one huge panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant background noise in my head where there’s always another thing to worry about.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things anymore. I still like the things I usually like when I actually get myself into them.

It’s more that sometimes I have a hard time fully settling into it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m watching something or doing something I normally enjoy, I might still laugh or still be interested, but part of me is kind of distracted or not completely relaxed. But I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone, exactly.

So… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change there. It’s not like I’ve stopped caring about everything I used to enjoy.

Like if I’m with family or watching something I like, I can still get into it and laugh or feel normal for a bit.\n\nI guess the tricky part is sometimes I’m just tired enough that starting anything feels like effort, so I might put things off or do less than I used to. But it’s not that the things feel empty or like I don’t care about them anymore. Once I’m there, I usually still enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or weighing myself every day or anything like that, but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought I’d noticeably gained or lost weight.\n\nMaybe it goes up or down a couple pounds like normal, depending on what I’ve eaten or how the week’s been, but nothing that’s stood out to me as a real change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or paying attention to things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some. It’s not like I can’t focus on anything at all, but my mind wanders when I’m trying to do something that should be pretty straightforward.\n\nLike at work, at the register, I’ll be looking right at the screen and doing the transaction, and then I realize I’ve kind of lost the thread because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever is hanging over me. Then I have to stop and double-check myself, or recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some small mistake before it becomes a bigger thing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’m there, I’m looking at it, but part of my brain is somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say I’ve been feeling anxious pretty often. It’s kind of like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for very long, but not in a good way. It jumps around from worry to worry.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, and then before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting stress on the house, and then I start worrying about my wife being stressed. Then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, or something I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into, “Am I falling behind? Am I letting people down?” And then at work I’ll start wondering if I’m messing something up or if people can tell I’m not fully on top of things.\n\nSo yeah, nervous is probably the right word. It’s not always one huge panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant background noise in my head where there’s always another thing to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things anymore. I still like the things I usually like when I actually get myself into them.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes I have a hard time fully settling into it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m watching something or doing something I normally enjoy, I might still laugh or still be interested, but part of me is kind of distracted or not completely relaxed. But I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change there. It’s not like I’ve stopped caring about everything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d put it that way. Yeah, definitely. It’s like I’ll think I’m done worrying about something, and then five minutes later I’m right back on it again, or I’ve jumped to something connected to it.

Money is a big one, but it doesn’t stay just money. It’ll turn into, “Are we okay this month?” and then, “What if something unexpected comes up?” And then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s making things harder at home, and then I worry my wife is carrying more stress than she’s saying. Then I’ll remember church stuff I said I’d help with, and I’ll start wondering if I’m dropping the ball there.

And at work, too… I’ll be at the register and start thinking, “Did I mess that up? Am I moving too slow? Can people tell I’m not totally focused?” It just kind of loops. It feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, but a lot of the same things keep coming back around.

It’s not like I can’t focus on anything at all, but my mind wanders when I’m trying to do something that should be pretty straightforward.\n\nLike at work, at the register, I’ll be looking right at the screen and doing the transaction, and then I realize I’ve kind of lost the thread because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever is hanging over me. I don’t know if I’d say trouble sitting still exactly. I mean, I’m not usually pacing around the room or anything like that. If I’m sitting, I can sit, especially if I’m supposed to be somewhere or I’m working or talking to somebody.

But I guess sometimes I notice I’m kind of shifting around, like adjusting in the chair or tapping my foot a little, and I don’t always realize I’m doing it until I catch myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by restless. It’s not like I feel like I’m going to jump out of my skin all the time. It’s more just… sometimes I have that keyed-up feeling and my body doesn’t feel fully relaxed.

So, yeah, maybe a little at times, but I wouldn’t say it’s one of the biggest things I’ve noticed.

It’s more general, I think. Like, it usually starts with something specific, but it doesn’t stay there very long. My mind kind of jumps around from one thing to the next.

I’ll be thinking about money, like whether we’re okay for the month, and then that turns into worrying about the relative staying with us and how that’s affecting the house. Then I start thinking about whether my wife is more stressed than she’s letting on. And then, almost like my brain is looking for the next thing, I’ll remember something I said I’d do for church and wonder if I’m behind on it or if I forgot something.

And work is another one. I’ll be at the register and start worrying, “Did I mess that up?” or “Am I going too slow?” or “Does it look like I don’t know what I’m doing?” So it’s not just one single thing. It’s like a bunch of things that keep rotating, and even if I settle one in my head for a minute, another one pops up.

Like if I’m watching something or doing something I normally enjoy, I might still laugh or still be interested, but part of me is kind of distracted or not completely relaxed. But I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change there. It’s not like I’ve stopped caring about everything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I mean, I have my moments where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, sure, but not like I’m down or empty most of the day every day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get tense or preoccupied, and then if something goes wrong I might feel low for a bit, but it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness sitting on me all day. I can still have normal parts of the day where I’m talking with people or doing what I need to do and I don’t feel empty.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the same things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s like I’ll think I’m done worrying about something, and then five minutes later I’m right back on it again, or I’ve jumped to something connected to it.\n\nMoney is a big one, but it doesn’t stay just money. It’ll turn into, “Are we okay this month?” and then, “What if something unexpected comes up?” And then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s making things harder at home, and then I worry my wife is carrying more stress than she’s saying. Then I’ll remember church stuff I said I’d help with, and I’ll start wondering if I’m dropping the ball there.\n\nAnd at work, too… I’ll be at the register and start thinking, “Did I mess that up? Am I moving too slow? Can people tell I’m not totally focused?” It just kind of loops. It feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, but a lot of the same things keep coming back around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt sad or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t really remember having a time where it was like that, where I was sad or empty most of the day, almost every day. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches, like anyone, where I felt discouraged or stressed and maybe more quiet than usual, but I don’t think it was that kind of constant emptiness you’re asking about.\n\nI guess I’d say if I’ve felt low before, it usually came and went with whatever was happening, not like it took over the whole day for days and days. So… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say trouble sitting still exactly. I mean, I’m not usually pacing around the room or anything like that. If I’m sitting, I can sit, especially if I’m supposed to be somewhere or I’m working or talking to somebody.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I notice I’m kind of shifting around, like adjusting in the chair or tapping my foot a little, and I don’t always realize I’m doing it until I catch myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by restless. It’s not like I feel like I’m going to jump out of my skin all the time. It’s more just… sometimes I have that keyed-up feeling and my body doesn’t feel fully relaxed.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe a little at times, but I wouldn’t say it’s one of the biggest things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing or is it more of a general feeling of worry that isn't focused on one particular issue?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still doing my job, I’m showing up and getting through the shifts, but I’ve noticed I don’t feel as confident at the register as I used to. I’ll make a small mistake, or think I might have, and then I end up double-checking the cash drawer more often than I probably need to. It slows me down some, and then I get more worried that I’m holding things up or that someone’s going to notice.

With daily stuff, it’s kind of the same. I still do what I need to do, but I’ve had to pull back on some things. Like I used to volunteer at the food pantry more, and I’ve cut back on that because I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything right now. It’s not that I don’t want to help, because I do, but when I look at work and home and everything else, adding that on top starts to feel like too much. So I guess it is affecting things, just not in a way where everything has fallen apart.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi… I guess the main reason I came in is I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. It’s like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body still feels heavy, and my head feels like it’s dragging before the day even starts.  \n\nI work as a cashier, so I’m on my feet and have to stay alert and talk to people, and lately when I get up for my shift it just feels harder than it should. Not like I stayed up all night or anything—more like I’m already tired before I’ve even done anything. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say sad, exactly. Not most of the time. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of discouraged or just fed up because I’m so tired and it’s hard to get through the day, but it doesn’t feel like I’m walking around miserable all the time or crying or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel drained, and then that makes me frustrated with myself, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Why is this so hard?” But I wouldn’t really describe my mood as sad most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically like to do, like hobbies or spending time with friends and family, about as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah? I still enjoy things when I actually get myself to do them. Like if I’m with family or watching something I like, I can still get into it and laugh or feel normal for a bit.\n\nI guess the tricky part is sometimes I’m just tired enough that starting anything feels like effort, so I might put things off or do less than I used to. But it’s not that the things feel empty or like I don’t care about them anymore. Once I’m there, I usually still enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or weighing myself every day or anything like that, but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought I’d noticeably gained or lost weight.\n\nMaybe it goes up or down a couple pounds like normal, depending on what I’ve eaten or how the week’s been, but nothing that’s stood out to me as a real change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or paying attention to things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some. It’s not like I can’t focus on anything at all, but my mind wanders when I’m trying to do something that should be pretty straightforward.\n\nLike at work, at the register, I’ll be looking right at the screen and doing the transaction, and then I realize I’ve kind of lost the thread because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever is hanging over me. Then I have to stop and double-check myself, or recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some small mistake before it becomes a bigger thing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’m there, I’m looking at it, but part of my brain is somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say I’ve been feeling anxious pretty often. It’s kind of like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for very long, but not in a good way. It jumps around from worry to worry.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, and then before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting stress on the house, and then I start worrying about my wife being stressed. Then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, or something I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into, “Am I falling behind? Am I letting people down?” And then at work I’ll start wondering if I’m messing something up or if people can tell I’m not fully on top of things.\n\nSo yeah, nervous is probably the right word. It’s not always one huge panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant background noise in my head where there’s always another thing to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things anymore. I still like the things I usually like when I actually get myself into them.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes I have a hard time fully settling into it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m watching something or doing something I normally enjoy, I might still laugh or still be interested, but part of me is kind of distracted or not completely relaxed. But I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change there. It’s not like I’ve stopped caring about everything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I mean, I have my moments where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, sure, but not like I’m down or empty most of the day every day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get tense or preoccupied, and then if something goes wrong I might feel low for a bit, but it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness sitting on me all day. I can still have normal parts of the day where I’m talking with people or doing what I need to do and I don’t feel empty.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the same things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s like I’ll think I’m done worrying about something, and then five minutes later I’m right back on it again, or I’ve jumped to something connected to it.\n\nMoney is a big one, but it doesn’t stay just money. It’ll turn into, “Are we okay this month?” and then, “What if something unexpected comes up?” And then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s making things harder at home, and then I worry my wife is carrying more stress than she’s saying. Then I’ll remember church stuff I said I’d help with, and I’ll start wondering if I’m dropping the ball there.\n\nAnd at work, too… I’ll be at the register and start thinking, “Did I mess that up? Am I moving too slow? Can people tell I’m not totally focused?” It just kind of loops. It feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, but a lot of the same things keep coming back around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt sad or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t really remember having a time where it was like that, where I was sad or empty most of the day, almost every day. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches, like anyone, where I felt discouraged or stressed and maybe more quiet than usual, but I don’t think it was that kind of constant emptiness you’re asking about.\n\nI guess I’d say if I’ve felt low before, it usually came and went with whatever was happening, not like it took over the whole day for days and days. So… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say trouble sitting still exactly. I mean, I’m not usually pacing around the room or anything like that. If I’m sitting, I can sit, especially if I’m supposed to be somewhere or I’m working or talking to somebody.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I notice I’m kind of shifting around, like adjusting in the chair or tapping my foot a little, and I don’t always realize I’m doing it until I catch myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by restless. It’s not like I feel like I’m going to jump out of my skin all the time. It’s more just… sometimes I have that keyed-up feeling and my body doesn’t feel fully relaxed.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe a little at times, but I wouldn’t say it’s one of the biggest things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing or is it more of a general feeling of worry that isn't focused on one particular issue?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Like, it usually starts with something specific, but it doesn’t stay there very long. My mind kind of jumps around from one thing to the next.\n\nI’ll be thinking about money, like whether we’re okay for the month, and then that turns into worrying about the relative staying with us and how that’s affecting the house. Then I start thinking about whether my wife is more stressed than she’s letting on. And then, almost like my brain is looking for the next thing, I’ll remember something I said I’d do for church and wonder if I’m behind on it or if I forgot something.\n\nAnd work is another one. I’ll be at the register and start worrying, “Did I mess that up?” or “Am I going too slow?” or “Does it look like I don’t know what I’m doing?” So it’s not just one single thing. It’s like a bunch of things that keep rotating, and even if I settle one in my head for a minute, another one pops up.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety affecting your ability to do your job as a cashier or handle daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still doing my job, I’m showing up and getting through the shifts, but I’ve noticed I don’t feel as confident at the register as I used to. I’ll make a small mistake, or think I might have, and then I end up double-checking the cash drawer more often than I probably need to. It slows me down some, and then I get more worried that I’m holding things up or that someone’s going to notice.\n\nWith daily stuff, it’s kind of the same. I still do what I need to do, but I’ve had to pull back on some things. Like I used to volunteer at the food pantry more, and I’ve cut back on that because I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything right now. It’s not that I don’t want to help, because I do, but when I look at work and home and everything else, adding that on top starts to feel like too much. So I guess it is affecting things, just not in a way where everything has fallen apart.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say trouble sitting still exactly. I mean, I’m not usually pacing around the room or anything like that. If I’m sitting, I can sit, especially if I’m supposed to be somewhere or I’m working or talking to somebody.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I notice I’m kind of shifting around, like adjusting in the chair or tapping my foot a little, and I don’t always realize I’m doing it until I catch myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by restless. It’s not like I feel like I’m going to jump out of my skin all the time. It’s more just… sometimes I have that keyed-up feeling and my body doesn’t feel fully relaxed.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe a little at times, but I wouldn’t say it’s one of the biggest things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing or is it more of a general feeling of worry that isn't focused on one particular issue?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Like, it usually starts with something specific, but it doesn’t stay there very long. My mind kind of jumps around from one thing to the next.\n\nI’ll be thinking about money, like whether we’re okay for the month, and then that turns into worrying about the relative staying with us and how that’s affecting the house. Then I start thinking about whether my wife is more stressed than she’s letting on. And then, almost like my brain is looking for the next thing, I’ll remember something I said I’d do for church and wonder if I’m behind on it or if I forgot something.\n\nAnd work is another one. I’ll be at the register and start worrying, “Did I mess that up?” or “Am I going too slow?” or “Does it look like I don’t know what I’m doing?” So it’s not just one single thing. It’s like a bunch of things that keep rotating, and even if I settle one in my head for a minute, another one pops up.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety affecting your ability to do your job as a cashier or handle daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still doing my job, I’m showing up and getting through the shifts, but I’ve noticed I don’t feel as confident at the register as I used to. I’ll make a small mistake, or think I might have, and then I end up double-checking the cash drawer more often than I probably need to. It slows me down some, and then I get more worried that I’m holding things up or that someone’s going to notice.\n\nWith daily stuff, it’s kind of the same. I still do what I need to do, but I’ve had to pull back on some things. Like I used to volunteer at the food pantry more, and I’ve cut back on that because I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything right now. It’s not that I don’t want to help, because I do, but when I look at work and home and everything else, adding that on top starts to feel like too much. So I guess it is affecting things, just not in a way where everything has fallen apart.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your anxiety affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / cashier / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can point to and say, “That was because of my anxiety.” My wife probably notices when I’m quieter or when I’m kind of in my own head, but we haven’t really had some major blowup over it.

With family, I guess I may be less relaxed sometimes. Like if there’s already a lot going on at home, I can be more on edge, but I don’t know if that’s affecting the relationship exactly or just making me not as easygoing as I’d like to be. I still talk to people, still show up for things when I can.

Friends… honestly, I don’t have a huge social circle anyway, so it’s hard to measure. I haven’t noticed people pulling away from me or anything. So I’d say maybe it affects how present I feel sometimes, but I can’t say it’s seriously damaging my relationships.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi… I guess the main reason I came in is I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. It’s like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body still feels heavy, and my head feels like it’s dragging before the day even starts.  \n\nI work as a cashier, so I’m on my feet and have to stay alert and talk to people, and lately when I get up for my shift it just feels harder than it should. Not like I stayed up all night or anything—more like I’m already tired before I’ve even done anything. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say sad, exactly. Not most of the time. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of discouraged or just fed up because I’m so tired and it’s hard to get through the day, but it doesn’t feel like I’m walking around miserable all the time or crying or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel drained, and then that makes me frustrated with myself, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Why is this so hard?” But I wouldn’t really describe my mood as sad most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically like to do, like hobbies or spending time with friends and family, about as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah? I still enjoy things when I actually get myself to do them. Like if I’m with family or watching something I like, I can still get into it and laugh or feel normal for a bit.\n\nI guess the tricky part is sometimes I’m just tired enough that starting anything feels like effort, so I might put things off or do less than I used to. But it’s not that the things feel empty or like I don’t care about them anymore. Once I’m there, I usually still enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or weighing myself every day or anything like that, but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought I’d noticeably gained or lost weight.\n\nMaybe it goes up or down a couple pounds like normal, depending on what I’ve eaten or how the week’s been, but nothing that’s stood out to me as a real change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or paying attention to things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some. It’s not like I can’t focus on anything at all, but my mind wanders when I’m trying to do something that should be pretty straightforward.\n\nLike at work, at the register, I’ll be looking right at the screen and doing the transaction, and then I realize I’ve kind of lost the thread because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever is hanging over me. Then I have to stop and double-check myself, or recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some small mistake before it becomes a bigger thing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’m there, I’m looking at it, but part of my brain is somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say I’ve been feeling anxious pretty often. It’s kind of like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for very long, but not in a good way. It jumps around from worry to worry.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, and then before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting stress on the house, and then I start worrying about my wife being stressed. Then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, or something I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into, “Am I falling behind? Am I letting people down?” And then at work I’ll start wondering if I’m messing something up or if people can tell I’m not fully on top of things.\n\nSo yeah, nervous is probably the right word. It’s not always one huge panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant background noise in my head where there’s always another thing to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things anymore. I still like the things I usually like when I actually get myself into them.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes I have a hard time fully settling into it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m watching something or doing something I normally enjoy, I might still laugh or still be interested, but part of me is kind of distracted or not completely relaxed. But I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change there. It’s not like I’ve stopped caring about everything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I mean, I have my moments where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, sure, but not like I’m down or empty most of the day every day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get tense or preoccupied, and then if something goes wrong I might feel low for a bit, but it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness sitting on me all day. I can still have normal parts of the day where I’m talking with people or doing what I need to do and I don’t feel empty.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the same things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s like I’ll think I’m done worrying about something, and then five minutes later I’m right back on it again, or I’ve jumped to something connected to it.\n\nMoney is a big one, but it doesn’t stay just money. It’ll turn into, “Are we okay this month?” and then, “What if something unexpected comes up?” And then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s making things harder at home, and then I worry my wife is carrying more stress than she’s saying. Then I’ll remember church stuff I said I’d help with, and I’ll start wondering if I’m dropping the ball there.\n\nAnd at work, too… I’ll be at the register and start thinking, “Did I mess that up? Am I moving too slow? Can people tell I’m not totally focused?” It just kind of loops. It feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, but a lot of the same things keep coming back around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt sad or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t really remember having a time where it was like that, where I was sad or empty most of the day, almost every day. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches, like anyone, where I felt discouraged or stressed and maybe more quiet than usual, but I don’t think it was that kind of constant emptiness you’re asking about.\n\nI guess I’d say if I’ve felt low before, it usually came and went with whatever was happening, not like it took over the whole day for days and days. So… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say trouble sitting still exactly. I mean, I’m not usually pacing around the room or anything like that. If I’m sitting, I can sit, especially if I’m supposed to be somewhere or I’m working or talking to somebody.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I notice I’m kind of shifting around, like adjusting in the chair or tapping my foot a little, and I don’t always realize I’m doing it until I catch myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by restless. It’s not like I feel like I’m going to jump out of my skin all the time. It’s more just… sometimes I have that keyed-up feeling and my body doesn’t feel fully relaxed.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe a little at times, but I wouldn’t say it’s one of the biggest things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing or is it more of a general feeling of worry that isn't focused on one particular issue?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Yeah, most nights, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body is ready to sleep, but then my mind doesn’t really shut off. I’ll start going over bills in my head, or thinking about my work schedule, or whether I’m doing enough at home, whether everyone in the house is okay… and it just kind of keeps circling.

Sometimes it’s not even one big thing. It’s like I lie there trying to settle down, and then one thought reminds me of another, and before I know it I’ve been awake longer than I meant to be. And even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t always feel like I really rested. I wake up feeling like I never fully relaxed, like my brain was still half-working all night.

It’s like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body still feels heavy, and my head feels like it’s dragging before the day even starts.  \n\nI work as a cashier, so I’m on my feet and have to stay alert and talk to people, and lately when I get up for my shift it just feels harder than it should. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say sad, exactly. Not most of the time. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of discouraged or just fed up because I’m so tired and it’s hard to get through the day, but it doesn’t feel like I’m walking around miserable all the time or crying or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel drained, and then that makes me frustrated with myself, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Why is this so hard?” But I wouldn’t really describe my mood as sad most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically like to do, like hobbies or spending time with friends and family, about as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah? I still enjoy things when I actually get myself to do them. Like if I’m with family or watching something I like, I can still get into it and laugh or feel normal for a bit.\n\nI guess the tricky part is sometimes I’m just tired enough that starting anything feels like effort, so I might put things off or do less than I used to. But it’s not that the things feel empty or like I don’t care about them anymore. Once I’m there, I usually still enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or weighing myself every day or anything like that, but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought I’d noticeably gained or lost weight.\n\nMaybe it goes up or down a couple pounds like normal, depending on what I’ve eaten or how the week’s been, but nothing that’s stood out to me as a real change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or paying attention to things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some. It’s not like I can’t focus on anything at all, but my mind wanders when I’m trying to do something that should be pretty straightforward.\n\nLike at work, at the register, I’ll be looking right at the screen and doing the transaction, and then I realize I’ve kind of lost the thread because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever is hanging over me. Then I have to stop and double-check myself, or recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some small mistake before it becomes a bigger thing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’m there, I’m looking at it, but part of my brain is somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say I’ve been feeling anxious pretty often. It’s kind of like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for very long, but not in a good way. It jumps around from worry to worry.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, and then before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting stress on the house, and then I start worrying about my wife being stressed. Then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, or something I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into, “Am I falling behind? Am I letting people down?” And then at work I’ll start wondering if I’m messing something up or if people can tell I’m not fully on top of things.\n\nSo yeah, nervous is probably the right word. It’s not always one huge panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant background noise in my head where there’s always another thing to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things anymore. I still like the things I usually like when I actually get myself into them.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes I have a hard time fully settling into it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m watching something or doing something I normally enjoy, I might still laugh or still be interested, but part of me is kind of distracted or not completely relaxed. But I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change there. It’s not like I’ve stopped caring about everything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I mean, I have my moments where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, sure, but not like I’m down or empty most of the day every day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get tense or preoccupied, and then if something goes wrong I might feel low for a bit, but it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness sitting on me all day. I can still have normal parts of the day where I’m talking with people or doing what I need to do and I don’t feel empty.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the same things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s like I’ll think I’m done worrying about something, and then five minutes later I’m right back on it again, or I’ve jumped to something connected to it.\n\nMoney is a big one, but it doesn’t stay just money. It’ll turn into, “Are we okay this month?” and then, “What if something unexpected comes up?” And then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s making things harder at home, and then I worry my wife is carrying more stress than she’s saying. Then I’ll remember church stuff I said I’d help with, and I’ll start wondering if I’m dropping the ball there.\n\nAnd at work, too… I’ll be at the register and start thinking, “Did I mess that up? Am I moving too slow? Can people tell I’m not totally focused?” It just kind of loops. It feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, but a lot of the same things keep coming back around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt sad or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t really remember having a time where it was like that, where I was sad or empty most of the day, almost every day. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches, like anyone, where I felt discouraged or stressed and maybe more quiet than usual, but I don’t think it was that kind of constant emptiness you’re asking about.\n\nI guess I’d say if I’ve felt low before, it usually came and went with whatever was happening, not like it took over the whole day for days and days. So… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say trouble sitting still exactly. I mean, I’m not usually pacing around the room or anything like that. If I’m sitting, I can sit, especially if I’m supposed to be somewhere or I’m working or talking to somebody.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I notice I’m kind of shifting around, like adjusting in the chair or tapping my foot a little, and I don’t always realize I’m doing it until I catch myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by restless. It’s not like I feel like I’m going to jump out of my skin all the time. It’s more just… sometimes I have that keyed-up feeling and my body doesn’t feel fully relaxed.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe a little at times, but I wouldn’t say it’s one of the biggest things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing or is it more of a general feeling of worry that isn't focused on one particular issue?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Like, it usually starts with something specific, but it doesn’t stay there very long. My mind kind of jumps around from one thing to the next.\n\nI’ll be thinking about money, like whether we’re okay for the month, and then that turns into worrying about the relative staying with us and how that’s affecting the house. Then I start thinking about whether my wife is more stressed than she’s letting on. And then, almost like my brain is looking for the next thing, I’ll remember something I said I’d do for church and wonder if I’m behind on it or if I forgot something.\n\nAnd work is another one. I’ll be at the register and start worrying, “Did I mess that up?” or “Am I going too slow?” or “Does it look like I don’t know what I’m doing?” So it’s not just one single thing. It’s like a bunch of things that keep rotating, and even if I settle one in my head for a minute, another one pops up.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety affecting your ability to do your job as a cashier or handle daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still doing my job, I’m showing up and getting through the shifts, but I’ve noticed I don’t feel as confident at the register as I used to. I’ll make a small mistake, or think I might have, and then I end up double-checking the cash drawer more often than I probably need to. It slows me down some, and then I get more worried that I’m holding things up or that someone’s going to notice.\n\nWith daily stuff, it’s kind of the same. I still do what I need to do, but I’ve had to pull back on some things. Like I used to volunteer at the food pantry more, and I’ve cut back on that because I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything right now. It’s not that I don’t want to help, because I do, but when I look at work and home and everything else, adding that on top starts to feel like too much. So I guess it is affecting things, just not in a way where everything has fallen apart.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can point to and say, “That was because of my anxiety.” My wife probably notices when I’m quieter or when I’m kind of in my own head, but we haven’t really had some major blowup over it.\n\nWith family, I guess I may be less relaxed sometimes. Like if there’s already a lot going on at home, I can be more on edge, but I don’t know if that’s affecting the relationship exactly or just making me not as easygoing as I’d like to be. I still talk to people, still show up for things when I can.\n\nFriends… honestly, I don’t have a huge social circle anyway, so it’s hard to measure. I haven’t noticed people pulling away from me or anything. So I’d say maybe it affects how present I feel sometimes, but I can’t say it’s seriously damaging my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself lying awake at night because you're worried about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, most nights, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body is ready to sleep, but then my mind doesn’t really shut off. I’ll start going over bills in my head, or thinking about my work schedule, or whether I’m doing enough at home, whether everyone in the house is okay… and it just kind of keeps circling.\n\nSometimes it’s not even one big thing. It’s like I lie there trying to settle down, and then one thought reminds me of another, and before I know it I’ve been awake longer than I meant to be. And even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t always feel like I really rested. I wake up feeling like I never fully relaxed, like my brain was still half-working all night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I’d say it does, but maybe not in a huge, dramatic way. I’m still doing the things I have to do, like going to work and taking care of regular responsibilities, but I don’t feel as free or comfortable doing them as I used to.

At work, for example, when I’m at the register, if I make even a small mistake—or honestly, if I just think I might have made one—I’ll end up double-checking the cash drawer more than I probably need to. And then that takes extra time, and I start feeling like I’m slowing everything down, which makes it harder to just get through the shift normally. So I’m participating, but I’m not really relaxed while I’m doing it.

And outside of work, I’ve cut back on volunteering at the food pantry. I used to do that more, and I liked feeling useful there, but lately it just feels like one more thing I have to keep up with, and I don’t feel like I have the room for it. So I guess it has taken away from some things I used to enjoy or feel good about. Not completely, but enough that I notice it.

I’m there, I’m looking at it, but part of my brain is somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say I’ve been feeling anxious pretty often. It’s kind of like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for very long, but not in a good way. It jumps around from worry to worry.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, and then before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting stress on the house, and then I start worrying about my wife being stressed. Then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, or something I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into, “Am I falling behind? Am I letting people down?” And then at work I’ll start wondering if I’m messing something up or if people can tell I’m not fully on top of things.\n\nSo yeah, nervous is probably the right word. It’s not always one huge panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant background noise in my head where there’s always another thing to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things anymore. I still like the things I usually like when I actually get myself into them.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes I have a hard time fully settling into it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m watching something or doing something I normally enjoy, I might still laugh or still be interested, but part of me is kind of distracted or not completely relaxed. But I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change there. It’s not like I’ve stopped caring about everything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I mean, I have my moments where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, sure, but not like I’m down or empty most of the day every day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get tense or preoccupied, and then if something goes wrong I might feel low for a bit, but it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness sitting on me all day. I can still have normal parts of the day where I’m talking with people or doing what I need to do and I don’t feel empty.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the same things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s like I’ll think I’m done worrying about something, and then five minutes later I’m right back on it again, or I’ve jumped to something connected to it.\n\nMoney is a big one, but it doesn’t stay just money. It’ll turn into, “Are we okay this month?” and then, “What if something unexpected comes up?” And then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s making things harder at home, and then I worry my wife is carrying more stress than she’s saying. Then I’ll remember church stuff I said I’d help with, and I’ll start wondering if I’m dropping the ball there.\n\nAnd at work, too… I’ll be at the register and start thinking, “Did I mess that up? Am I moving too slow? Can people tell I’m not totally focused?” It just kind of loops. It feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, but a lot of the same things keep coming back around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt sad or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t really remember having a time where it was like that, where I was sad or empty most of the day, almost every day. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches, like anyone, where I felt discouraged or stressed and maybe more quiet than usual, but I don’t think it was that kind of constant emptiness you’re asking about.\n\nI guess I’d say if I’ve felt low before, it usually came and went with whatever was happening, not like it took over the whole day for days and days. So… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say trouble sitting still exactly. I mean, I’m not usually pacing around the room or anything like that. If I’m sitting, I can sit, especially if I’m supposed to be somewhere or I’m working or talking to somebody.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I notice I’m kind of shifting around, like adjusting in the chair or tapping my foot a little, and I don’t always realize I’m doing it until I catch myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by restless. It’s not like I feel like I’m going to jump out of my skin all the time. It’s more just… sometimes I have that keyed-up feeling and my body doesn’t feel fully relaxed.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe a little at times, but I wouldn’t say it’s one of the biggest things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing or is it more of a general feeling of worry that isn't focused on one particular issue?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Like, it usually starts with something specific, but it doesn’t stay there very long. My mind kind of jumps around from one thing to the next.\n\nI’ll be thinking about money, like whether we’re okay for the month, and then that turns into worrying about the relative staying with us and how that’s affecting the house. Then I start thinking about whether my wife is more stressed than she’s letting on. And then, almost like my brain is looking for the next thing, I’ll remember something I said I’d do for church and wonder if I’m behind on it or if I forgot something.\n\nAnd work is another one. I’ll be at the register and start worrying, “Did I mess that up?” or “Am I going too slow?” or “Does it look like I don’t know what I’m doing?” So it’s not just one single thing. It’s like a bunch of things that keep rotating, and even if I settle one in my head for a minute, another one pops up.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety affecting your ability to do your job as a cashier or handle daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still doing my job, I’m showing up and getting through the shifts, but I’ve noticed I don’t feel as confident at the register as I used to. I’ll make a small mistake, or think I might have, and then I end up double-checking the cash drawer more often than I probably need to. It slows me down some, and then I get more worried that I’m holding things up or that someone’s going to notice.\n\nWith daily stuff, it’s kind of the same. I still do what I need to do, but I’ve had to pull back on some things. Like I used to volunteer at the food pantry more, and I’ve cut back on that because I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything right now. It’s not that I don’t want to help, because I do, but when I look at work and home and everything else, adding that on top starts to feel like too much. So I guess it is affecting things, just not in a way where everything has fallen apart.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I don’t think it’s caused any big fights or anything like that, at least not that I can point to and say, “That was because of my anxiety.” My wife probably notices when I’m quieter or when I’m kind of in my own head, but we haven’t really had some major blowup over it.\n\nWith family, I guess I may be less relaxed sometimes. Like if there’s already a lot going on at home, I can be more on edge, but I don’t know if that’s affecting the relationship exactly or just making me not as easygoing as I’d like to be. I still talk to people, still show up for things when I can.\n\nFriends… honestly, I don’t have a huge social circle anyway, so it’s hard to measure. I haven’t noticed people pulling away from me or anything. So I’d say maybe it affects how present I feel sometimes, but I can’t say it’s seriously damaging my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself lying awake at night because you're worried about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, most nights, honestly. I’ll be tired when I get into bed, like my body is ready to sleep, but then my mind doesn’t really shut off. I’ll start going over bills in my head, or thinking about my work schedule, or whether I’m doing enough at home, whether everyone in the house is okay… and it just kind of keeps circling.\n\nSometimes it’s not even one big thing. It’s like I lie there trying to settle down, and then one thought reminds me of another, and before I know it I’ve been awake longer than I meant to be. And even when I do fall asleep, it doesn’t always feel like I really rested. I wake up feeling like I never fully relaxed, like my brain was still half-working all night.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is significantly impacting your ability to enjoy or participate in daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say it does, but maybe not in a huge, dramatic way. I’m still doing the things I have to do, like going to work and taking care of regular responsibilities, but I don’t feel as free or comfortable doing them as I used to.\n\nAt work, for example, when I’m at the register, if I make even a small mistake—or honestly, if I just think I might have made one—I’ll end up double-checking the cash drawer more than I probably need to. And then that takes extra time, and I start feeling like I’m slowing everything down, which makes it harder to just get through the shift normally. So I’m participating, but I’m not really relaxed while I’m doing it.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve cut back on volunteering at the food pantry. I used to do that more, and I liked feeling useful there, but lately it just feels like one more thing I have to keep up with, and I don’t feel like I have the room for it. So I guess it has taken away from some things I used to enjoy or feel good about. Not completely, but enough that I notice it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So I’m participating, but I’m not really relaxed while I’m doing it.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve cut back on volunteering at the food pantry. I used to do that more, and I liked feeling useful there, but lately it just feels like one more thing I have to keep up with, and I don’t feel like I have the room for it. So I guess it has taken away from some things I used to enjoy or feel good about. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, somewhat. I wouldn’t say I’m hiding from everything or refusing to go places, but I do find myself pulling back from things when they feel like they’re going to add more pressure.

At work, it’s not exactly avoiding the register, because that’s my job and I still do it, but if I make a small mistake—or even think I might have—I’ll double-check the cash drawer more than I probably need to. It’s like I don’t trust myself to just move on. And then I get slower, and then I worry I’m holding things up, so it kind of becomes this whole loop.

The bigger example is volunteering at the food pantry. I used to do that more regularly, and I actually liked it. It felt good to help out and be useful. But lately I’ve cut back because it just feels like one more thing I have to keep track of, one more place I have to show up and do things right, and I don’t feel like I can keep up with everything. So I guess, yeah, I am avoiding or backing away from some activities—not completely, but enough that I notice it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi… I guess the main reason I came in is I’ve just been feeling really worn out almost every day. It’s like, even if I get what should be a full night’s sleep, I wake up and my body still feels heavy, and my head feels like it’s dragging before the day even starts.  \n\nI work as a cashier, so I’m on my feet and have to stay alert and talk to people, and lately when I get up for my shift it just feels harder than it should. Not like I stayed up all night or anything—more like I’m already tired before I’ve even done anything. Not most of the time. I mean, I have moments where I feel kind of discouraged or just fed up because I’m so tired and it’s hard to get through the day, but it doesn’t feel like I’m walking around miserable all the time or crying or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel drained, and then that makes me frustrated with myself, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Why is this so hard?” But I wouldn’t really describe my mood as sad most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you typically like to do, like hobbies or spending time with friends and family, about as much as you used to?\n\nPatient: I think… mostly, yeah? I still enjoy things when I actually get myself to do them. Like if I’m with family or watching something I like, I can still get into it and laugh or feel normal for a bit.\n\nI guess the tricky part is sometimes I’m just tired enough that starting anything feels like effort, so I might put things off or do less than I used to. Once I’m there, I usually still enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven’t been tracking it closely or weighing myself every day or anything like that, but my clothes fit about the same, and I haven’t looked in the mirror and thought I’d noticeably gained or lost weight.\n\nMaybe it goes up or down a couple pounds like normal, depending on what I’ve eaten or how the week’s been, but nothing that’s stood out to me as a real change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating or paying attention to things lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have noticed that some. It’s not like I can’t focus on anything at all, but my mind wanders when I’m trying to do something that should be pretty straightforward.\n\nLike at work, at the register, I’ll be looking right at the screen and doing the transaction, and then I realize I’ve kind of lost the thread because I’m thinking about stuff at home or bills or whatever is hanging over me. Then I have to stop and double-check myself, or recount the drawer, or I’ll catch some small mistake before it becomes a bigger thing.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’m there, I’m looking at it, but part of my brain is somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or nervous lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’d say I’ve been feeling anxious pretty often. It’s kind of like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing for very long, but not in a good way. It jumps around from worry to worry.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about money, and then before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s putting stress on the house, and then I start worrying about my wife being stressed. Then I remember something I said I’d help with at church, or something I’m supposed to do, and then it turns into, “Am I falling behind? Am I letting people down?” And then at work I’ll start wondering if I’m messing something up or if people can tell I’m not fully on top of things.\n\nSo yeah, nervous is probably the right word. It’s not always one huge panic feeling or anything, it’s more like this constant background noise in my head where there’s always another thing to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're able to find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I can’t enjoy things anymore. I still like the things I usually like when I actually get myself into them.\n\nIt’s more that sometimes I have a hard time fully settling into it, if that makes sense. Like if I’m watching something or doing something I normally enjoy, I might still laugh or still be interested, but part of me is kind of distracted or not completely relaxed. But I wouldn’t say the pleasure is gone, exactly.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a big change there. It’s not like I’ve stopped caring about everything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’d put it that way. I mean, I have my moments where I feel worn out or kind of discouraged, sure, but not like I’m down or empty most of the day every day.\n\nIt’s more like… I get tense or preoccupied, and then if something goes wrong I might feel low for a bit, but it doesn’t feel like this constant sadness sitting on me all day. I can still have normal parts of the day where I’m talking with people or doing what I need to do and I don’t feel empty.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed that nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about the same things over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s like I’ll think I’m done worrying about something, and then five minutes later I’m right back on it again, or I’ve jumped to something connected to it.\n\nMoney is a big one, but it doesn’t stay just money. It’ll turn into, “Are we okay this month?” and then, “What if something unexpected comes up?” And then I’ll start thinking about the relative staying with us, and whether that’s making things harder at home, and then I worry my wife is carrying more stress than she’s saying. Then I’ll remember church stuff I said I’d help with, and I’ll start wondering if I’m dropping the ball there.\n\nAnd at work, too… I’ll be at the register and start thinking, “Did I mess that up? Am I moving too slow? Can people tell I’m not totally focused?” It just kind of loops. It feels like my mind is always jumping from one thing to the next, but a lot of the same things keep coming back around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt sad or empty for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t really remember having a time where it was like that, where I was sad or empty most of the day, almost every day. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches, like anyone, where I felt discouraged or stressed and maybe more quiet than usual, but I don’t think it was that kind of constant emptiness you’re asking about.\n\nI guess I’d say if I’ve felt low before, it usually came and went with whatever was happening, not like it took over the whole day for days and days. So… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say trouble sitting still exactly. I mean, I’m not usually pacing around the room or anything like that. If I’m sitting, I can sit, especially if I’m supposed to be somewhere or I’m working or talking to somebody.\n\nBut I guess sometimes I notice I’m kind of shifting around, like adjusting in the chair or tapping my foot a little, and I don’t always realize I’m doing it until I catch myself. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean by restless. It’s not like I feel like I’m going to jump out of my skin all the time. It’s more just… sometimes I have that keyed-up feeling and my body doesn’t feel fully relaxed.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe a little at times, but I wouldn’t say it’s one of the biggest things I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific thing or is it more of a general feeling of worry that isn't focused on one particular issue?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. Like, it usually starts with something specific, but it doesn’t stay there very long. My mind kind of jumps around from one thing to the next.\n\nI’ll be thinking about money, like whether we’re okay for the month, and then that turns into worrying about the relative staying with us and how that’s affecting the house. Then I start thinking about whether my wife is more stressed than she’s letting on. And then, almost like my brain is looking for the next thing, I’ll remember something I said I’d do for church and wonder if I’m behind on it or if I forgot something.\n\nAnd work is another one. I’ll be at the register and start worrying, “Did I mess that up?” or “Am I going too slow?” or “Does it look like I don’t know what I’m doing?” So it’s not just one single thing. It’s like a bunch of things that keep rotating, and even if I settle one in my head for a minute, another one pops up.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety affecting your ability to do your job as a cashier or handle daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. I mean, I’m still doing my job, I’m showing up and getting through the shifts, but I’ve noticed I don’t feel as confident at the register as I used to. I’ll make a small mistake, or think I might have, and then I end up double-checking the cash drawer more often than I probably need to. It slows me down some, and then I get more worried that I’m holding things up or that someone’s going to notice.\n\nWith daily stuff, it’s kind of the same. Like I used to volunteer at the food pantry more, and I’ve cut back on that because I just feel like I can’t keep up with everything right now.
